&lt;/project&gt;</file><file path="java/opentron-java/confidential-worker/README.md"># CJAE Confidential Worker

This module builds the standalone confidential gateway worker. It compiles the
confidential memory, GCP attestation, OIDC gateway, and ONNX inference packages
without compiling or packaging the wider OpenTron backend.

Build from the repository root:

```powershell
mvn -f java/opentron-java/confidential-worker/pom.xml clean package
docker build -f java/opentron-java/backend/Dockerfile.confidential `
  -t cjae-worker:worker-only .
```

The image starts with `gcp-cvm,onnx-pii`; the public gateway remains disabled
unless explicitly enabled with the `gateway-oidc` profile and
`CONFIDENTIAL_GATEWAY_ENABLED=true`. ONNX Runtime and the pinned model load
independently of Ollama.

## GCP gateway deployment prerequisites

The VM startup script constructs `/run/cjae/worker.env` from instance
metadata, then passes it to Docker. Only public OIDC settings and the
KMS-encrypted context DEK are stored in metadata; never store a plaintext
context key or bearer token there. Required settings are:

```text
GATEWAY_OIDC_ISSUER=https://identity-provider.example/
GATEWAY_OIDC_JWKS_URI=https://identity-provider.example/.well-known/jwks.json
GATEWAY_OIDC_AUDIENCE=cjae-confidential-gateway
GATEWAY_OIDC_TENANT_CLAIM=tenant_id
GATEWAY_OIDC_FIXED_SCOPES=
GCP_WIF_PROVIDER_AUDIENCE=//iam.googleapis.com/projects/PROJECT_NUMBER/locations/global/workloadIdentityPools/POOL/providers/PROVIDER
GCP_KMS_KEY_RESOURCE=projects/PROJECT_ID/locations/LOCATION/keyRings/KEY_RING/cryptoKeys/KEY
GCP_CONTEXT_KEY_ID=key-version-id
GCP_CONTEXT_DEK_CIPHERTEXT_BASE64=BASE64_ENCODED_KMS_CIPHERTEXT
```

These are deployment placeholders, not configured production values. The
OIDC issuer, signing keys, audience, WIF provider, KMS key policy, and
encrypted 256-bit context key must be supplied and verified by the deployment
owner. The existing WIF provider must use the GCA token&apos;s verified issuer and
an attribute condition restricted to the approved GCE project, zone, instance,
hardware model, and secure-boot claims. Do not enable key release until those
conditions have been tested on the target Confidential VM.

When `GATEWAY_OIDC_FIXED_SCOPES` is non-empty, the listed recognized scopes
are granted to every valid token for the configured issuer and audience;
the token does not need a scopes claim. Configure this only when that
server-side authorization policy is intentional. Google ID tokens can use
`GATEWAY_OIDC_TENANT_CLAIM=sub` to isolate secure contexts by stable user
subject, with fixed scopes explicitly set for the desired gateway operations.

The startup script intentionally fails before replacing the running container
if any required metadata value is absent. Configure and
validate the runtime service account, vTPM access, image permissions, and
network policy separately before deploying.

The local ONNX smoke test validates packaging, model loading, and the HTTP
health route only. Local static-key mode is an untrusted development profile;
it is not evidence of GCP attestation or production key release.

The GCP adapter obtains evidence with Google&apos;s `gotpm` utility rather than a
direct Panama binding to `/dev/sev-guest` or `/dev/tdx-guest`. Direct
provider-specific Panama bindings and the AWS Nitro/Azure adapters are not
included in this worker artifact.</file><file path="java/opentron-java/CONFIDENTIAL_AI_GATEWAY_ARCHITECTURE.md">CONFIDENTIAL JAVA AI ENGINE (CJAE) ARCHITECTURE SPECIFICATION


MULTI-CLOUD BLUEPRINT FOR HARDWARE-ENFORCED CONFIDENTIAL AI
THIS EXPLAINS THE BEHAVIOR TO BE IMPLEMENTED AND SHOULD NOT BE MODIFIED !


1. PURPOSE AND PARADIGM

The CONFIDENTIAL JAVA AI ENGINE (CJAE) is a production-grade, type-safe, high-concurrency architecture that implements specialized Confidential AI capabilities natively on the Java Virtual Machine (JVM). CJAE acts as a secure, zero-trust overlay for enterprise AI workloads. Its primary objective is to guarantee end-to-end data privacy by ensuring that sensitive data, LLM weights, tokenization maps, and vector arrays remain fully encrypted even during active computation.
By executing directly inside hardware-isolated Trusted Execution Environments (TEEs), CJAE isolates data in use from host operators, cloud providers, and guest OS vulnerabilities. Unlike Python-based confidential computing layers that suffer from high serialization latency, CJAE weaponizes modern JVM capabilities to deliver hardware-enforced privacy at native execution speeds.

2. ARCHITECTURAL VALIDATION &amp; THE BEST APPROACH

To ensure this specification translates into a secure, working implementation, the architectural design explicitly addresses known hardware and runtime constraints through the following engineering approaches:
• The TEE Boundary Approach: Project Panama and MemorySegment do not natively modify the underlying hardware state or auto-create enclaves. Therefore, CJAE does not rely on Java to spin up the TEE. The best approach is an Infrastructure-First deployment model, where the entire JVM/GraalVM binary is booted directly inside a pre-configured hardware-encrypted environment managed by cloud-specific orchestrators. Java code interacts with this existing environment strictly via file descriptors, virtual sockets, and verified native libraries.
• The Hardware Abstraction Approach: AMD SEV-SNP (which encrypts an entire virtual machine), Intel SGX/TDX, and AWS Nitro Enclaves utilize entirely different execution, driver, and attestation models. One generic native binding cannot implement them all. The best approach is a pluggable Service Provider Interface (SPI) pattern. CJAE decouples the core engine from the cloud hardware layer by defining clean, abstract interfaces for attestation, transport, and key release. Cloud-specific provider modules are compiled as decoupled adapters and loaded dynamically based on the active deployment environment profile.
• The Execution Guard Approach: The @ConfidentialAI annotation cannot automatically warp a thread into a TEE or safely parse arbitrary, deeply nested object arguments. The best approach is an Isolated Gateway Architecture. @ConfidentialAI is used purely as an execution guard to intercept calls, block unauthorized states, and enforce a fail-closed policy. It passes execution to explicit, low-level service boundaries handling flat primitive data types (like byte arrays and float arrays) that can be predictably monitored, mapped, and scrubbed.
• The Identity &amp; Multi-Tenancy Approach: Managing raw database-bound tenant API keys inside the confidential worker violates zero-trust principles and increases state bloat within the TEE. The best approach is an OIDC/JWT Token Validation &amp; Decoupled Architecture. CJAE leverages signed OIDC bearer tokens containing cryptographically verified multi-tenant context, scopes, and quota boundaries. The engine extracts these attributes off-heap using standard verification keys (JWKS), completely decoupling the TEE worker from central identity databases while enforcing hard data isolation.
• The Compilation Compatibility Approach: Ahead-of-Time (AOT) compilation via GraalVM Native Image, dynamic JNI behavior within the ONNX Runtime, and hardware-enforced TEE constraints introduce severe runtime stability risks if unverified. The best approach is a Rigid Automated Validation Pipeline. The build pipeline executes a mandatory conformance test suite inside real target cloud TEE instances prior to release approval, ensuring reflection configurations (reflect-config.json) and shared native libraries are fully mapped and stable.
• The Memory &amp; Zeroization Approach: Using Virtual Threads, Project Panama, and off-heap allocations does not inherently guarantee zero-copy performance or safe data destruction. Plaintext leaks can occur if arrays are duplicated on the heap during standard object transitions. The best approach is Mandated Off-Heap Lifecycle Management. CJAE restricts sensitive data structures exclusively to Panama-allocated MemorySegment blocks. These blocks are guarded by strict try-with-resources blocks that execute explicit hardware-accelerated memory-zeroing loops (internalSegment.fill((byte) 0)) immediately upon thread completion or process failure.

3. THE MULTI-CLOUD ARCHITECTURAL MATRIX

To achieve true cross-cloud deployment without polluting the core AI pipelines, CJAE splits its attestation and transport boundary into isolated, environment-driven modules.
                  +-------------------------------------------------+

                  |      CJAE Core / @ConfidentialAI Aspect Proxy   |
                  +------------------------|------------------------+
                                           | (SPI Decoupling)
                                           v
      +------------------------------------+------------------------------------+

      |                                    |                                    |
      v                                    v                                    v
+----------------------------+     +----------------------------+     +----------------------------+

|    Azure CVM Adapter       |     |      GCP CVM Adapter       |     |     AWS Nitro Adapter      |
|  - AMD SEV-SNP / TDX       |     |  - AMD SEV-SNP / TDX       |     |  - Micro-VM Partition      |
|  - Read: /dev/sev-guest    |     |  - Read: /dev/sev-guest    |     |  - Native: libnsm.so       |
|  - Auth: Azure Attestation |     |  - Auth: GCP TEE API       |     |  - Transport: Vsock IPC    |
+----------------------------+     +----------------------------+     +----------------------------+

3.1 Azure Confidential VM Module (cjae-attestation-azure-sev)

• Isolation Paradigm: Confidential VM (CVM) providing full memory and guest operating system encryption via AMD SEV-SNP or Intel TDX.
• Panama Binding Target: Low-level Linux character devices located at /dev/sev-guest or /dev/tdx-guest.
• Attestation Mechanism: Fetches unforgeable hardware-signed quotes via Panama driver interactions, constructs a standardized payload, and posts it to the Azure Attestation Service.
• Key Release Boundary: Validated policy tokens are submitted directly to Azure Key Vault / Managed HSM to release ephemeral Data Encryption Keys (DEKs) into the guest runtime memory.

3.2 Google Cloud Confidential VM Module (cjae-attestation-gcp-sev)

• Isolation Paradigm: Confidential VM architecture enforcing hardware-level page table and cache insulation using AMD SEV-SNP or Intel TDX.
• Panama Binding Target: Kernel-exposed file descriptors mapped to the guest platform architecture.
• Attestation Mechanism: Collects hardware evidence and routes requests natively to the GCP Confidential Computing Enforcement API.
• Key Release Boundary: Integrates directly with Google Cloud KMS to dynamically release cryptographic keys after validating the system image measurements.

3.3 AWS Nitro Enclave Module (cjae-attestation-aws-nitro)

• Isolation Paradigm: CPU and Memory isolation micro-VM. It features an completely isolated environment with no persistent disk, no external internet ingress, and no traditional OS stack.
• Panama Binding Target: Binds to the native AWS Nitro Secure Module library (libnsm.so) using custom SymbolLookup configurations.
• Transport Mechanism: Since typical TCP/HTTP networking is blocked inside the enclave, this adapter switches communication entirely to virtual machine sockets (vsock). Data is ingested from the parent EC2 host via bidirectional IPC streams.
• Attestation &amp; Key Release Boundary: The Panama binding queries the Nitro Secure Module to generate a cryptographic enclave document. This document is tunneled through the vsock directly to AWS KMS. AWS KMS evaluates the hardware image hashes natively and returns decrypted key bytes straight into the enclave&apos;s memory space.

4. THREE-STEP IMPLEMENTATION PIPELINE


STEP 1: THE TEE BINDING (NATIVE HARDWARE ACCESS)

CJAE bypasses the legacy Java Native Interface (JNI) to eliminate translation layer latency. It uses Project Panama (Foreign Function &amp; Memory API) to build a lightweight, type-safe Java wrapper directly around target cloud drivers (/dev/sev-guest) or proprietary enclave libraries (libnsm.so).
By leveraging the Panama Linker and SymbolLookup subsystems, the engine maps native memory pointers and loads target hardware guest device file descriptors or libraries natively. This allows Java applications to execute low-level hardware operations—such as allocating encrypted pages, fetching unforgeable platform hashes, and running vector math over TEE-protected caches—at near-native C-speeds.
+------------------------------------------------------------+

|             Confidential Java AI Engine Runtime            |
|  [Arena Memory]  ---&gt;  [Panama FFM API MemorySegment]      |
+-------------------------------|----------------------------+
                                | (Zero-Copy SPI Dispatch)
                                v
     +--------------------------+--------------------------+

     | (Confidential VMs)                                  | (AWS Nitro Enclaves)
     v                                                     v
+------------------------------------+             +------------------------------+

|   Kernel Guest Driver Interface    |             |  Nitro Secure Module Library |
| [/dev/sev-guest] / [/dev/tdx-guest]|             |        [libnsm.so via Panama]|
+------------------------------------+             +------------------------------+

STEP 2: MICRO-FRAMEWORK INTEGRATION (DECLARATIVE MIDDLEWARE)

CJAE provides a secure middleware layer designed to snap seamlessly into enterprise ecosystems like Spring AI or LangChain4j. Instead of requiring developers to write low-level cryptographic code or establish standalone Python sidecars, CJAE introduces a declarative, proxy-interceptable annotation: @ConfidentialAI.
When applied to a Spring Boot service, repository layer, or LangChain4j tool agent, a Spring AOP proxy intercepts execution. The aspect instantly handles prompt tokenization, routes the specific tensor payloads to off-heap Arena memory, and processes the AI logic within the secure hardware enclave. If the underlying hardware verifier detects an expired lease or unauthenticated state, the middleware triggers a fail-closed execution halt before any data can leak to the host.
[ Incoming Application Request ]
               |
               v
  @ConfidentialAI Aspect Proxy
               |
      (Fails Closed if Unverified or Multi-Tenant Token Fails Validation)
               v
+--------------------------------------------+

|        Spring AI / LangChain4j Context     |
|  - Local NLP Tokenizer Masks Entities      |
|  - Off-Heap Memory Transmitted to Enclave  |
+--------------------------------------------+

STEP 3: ATTESTATION ENGINE (JAVA-NATIVE CRYPTOGRAPHIC VERIFICATION)

CJAE features a built-in, Java-native cryptographic attestation service designed to build complete client-side trust. When an external client application initiates a nonce challenge or transmits sensitive payloads, the Attestation Engine queries the underlying hardware layer established in Step 1 to extract raw, unforgeable hardware measurements.
The engine processes this raw evidence against explicit provider-specific roots of trust (such as Azure Attestation, GCP Enforcement API, or AWS KMS). It returns a cryptographic, hardware-signed attestation bundle containing service identity assertions, build measurements, policy versions, and platform claims. This bundle serves as public cryptographic evidence proving to the client that the code running inside the JVM has not been tampered with, is executing on verified hardware, and is completely isolated from host intervention.
Client App                   CJAE Gateway / TEE              Attestation Provider

    |                               |                                |
    |----- 1. Nonce Challenge -----&gt;|                                |
    |                               |----- 2. Query Driver/NSM -----&gt;|
    |                               |&lt;---- 3. Raw Evidence Report ---|
    |                               |                                |
    |                               |----- 4. Submit Evidence ------&gt;|
    |                               |&lt;---- 5. Signed Policy Claims --|
    |&lt;---- 6. Attestation Bundle ---|                                |

5. AOT COMPILATION &amp; ENCLAVE DEPLOYMENT MATRIX

To fit within the memory-constrained bounds of hardware enclaves, the application footprint is stripped of standard dynamic JVM components. The system uses GraalVM Native Image to compile the code ahead-of-time (AOT) into a standalone native binary that eliminates reflection, dynamic class loading, and standard JVM management bloat.

Maven Profile Build Configuration (pom.xml)

xml
&lt;profile&gt;
    &lt;id&gt;native-confidential&lt;/id&gt;
    &lt;build&gt;
        &lt;plugins&gt;
            &lt;plugin&gt;
                &lt;groupId&gt;org.graalvm.buildtools&lt;/groupId&gt;
                &lt;artifactId&gt;native-maven-plugin&lt;/artifactId&gt;
                &lt;version&gt;0.10.1&lt;/version&gt;
                &lt;extensions&gt;true&lt;/extensions&gt;
                &lt;configuration&gt;
                    &lt;imageName&gt;confidential-java-ai-worker&lt;/imageName&gt;
                    &lt;mainClass&gt;org.cjae.engine.MainApplication&lt;/mainClass&gt;
                    &lt;buildArgs&gt;
                        &lt;!-- Permit runtime native execution for Project Panama hooks --&gt;
                        &lt;buildArg&gt;--enable-native-access=org.cjae.engine&lt;/buildArg&gt;
                        &lt;!-- Strip JVM fallback modes to guarantee a strict zero-dependency binary --&gt;
                        &lt;buildArg&gt;--no-fallback&lt;/buildArg&gt;
                        &lt;!-- Enforce maximum optimization for execution speed and memory limits --&gt;
                        &lt;buildArg&gt;-O3&lt;/buildArg&gt;
                        &lt;buildArg&gt;-H:+AllowVMParsing&lt;/buildArg&gt;
                    &lt;/buildArgs&gt;
                &lt;/configuration&gt;
            &lt;/plugin&gt;
        &lt;/plugins&gt;
    &lt;/build&gt;
&lt;/profile&gt;
Use code with caution.

Ahead-of-Time JNI Metadata Target (reflect-config.json)

This configuration explicitly preserves the necessary JNI structures required by the ONNX Runtime Java API, ensuring they are not stripped away by GraalVM dead-code elimination loops:
json
[
  {
    &quot;name&quot;: &quot;ai.onnxruntime.OrtEnvironment&quot;,
    &quot;allDeclaredConstructors&quot;: true,
    &quot;allPublicMethods&quot;: true,
    &quot;allDeclaredFields&quot;: true
  },
  {
    &quot;name&quot;: &quot;ai.onnxruntime.OrtSession&quot;,
    &quot;allDeclaredConstructors&quot;: true,
    &quot;allPublicMethods&quot;: true,
    &quot;allDeclaredFields&quot;: true
  },
  {
    &quot;name&quot;: &quot;ai.onnxruntime.OnnxTensor&quot;,
    &quot;allDeclaredConstructors&quot;: true,
    &quot;allPublicMethods&quot;: true,
    &quot;allDeclaredFields&quot;: true
  },
  {
    &quot;name&quot;: &quot;java.nio.DirectByteBuffer&quot;,
    &quot;allDeclaredConstructors&quot;: true,
    &quot;allPublicMethods&quot;: true,
    &quot;allDeclaredFields&quot;: true
  }
]
Use code with caution.

6. THREAT MODELING AND RIGID CONTROLS


Threat Vector	CJAE Architectural Control Pattern	Residual System Risk
Host Operator / Hypervisor Memory Snoop	Strict hardware-level cache and page table encryption (SEV-SNP, TDX, Nitro isolation) masks runtime data dynamically.	Speculative execution bugs or side-channel exploits in CPU microcode architectures.
Tampered / Compromised System Workloads	Attestation Engine binds image measurements to the hardware boot sequence, blocking key release on policy mismatch.	Pre-compilation supply chain vulnerabilities or malicious third-party open-source dependencies.
JVM Heap Dumps / Core File Extraction	Hard migration of all active AI weights, contexts, and arrays out of the JVM heap into Panama off-heap storage.	Unmanaged native code running with broad process clearance within the same enclave boundary.
Cross-Tenant Context Contamination	Multi-tenant OIDC JWT context parsing enforces structural runtime segmentation across executing context handles.	Tenant configuration flaws or logical token signing exploit profiles.
Concurrency Latency Spikes / Heap Spills	Project Loom virtual threads pair with GC-immune Arena layouts, keeping latency charts flat under load.	Delayed execution triggers or un-scrubbed native buffers if an abrupt process panic occurs.</file><file path="startup_script.sh">#!/bin/bash
set -euo pipefail

readonly REGISTRY=&quot;us-central1-docker.pkg.dev&quot;
readonly IMAGE=&quot;us-central1-docker.pkg.dev/cjae-confidential-engine/cjae-repo/cjae-worker:latest&quot;
readonly METADATA=&quot;http://metadata.google.internal/computeMetadata/v1&quot;
readonly GATEWAY_ENV_FILE=&quot;/run/cjae/worker.env&quot;
readonly TPM_EVENT_LOG=&quot;/run/cjae/tpm-event-log&quot;
TPM_DEVICE=&quot;/dev/tpmrm0&quot;

metadata_value() {
  curl --fail --silent --show-error \
    -H &quot;Metadata-Flavor: Google&quot; \
    &quot;${METADATA}/$1&quot;
}

write_gateway_environment() {
  install -d -m 0700 /run/cjae
  umask 077
  : &gt; &quot;${GATEWAY_ENV_FILE}&quot;

  for mapping in \
    &quot;GATEWAY_OIDC_ISSUER:cjae-gateway-oidc-issuer&quot; \
    &quot;GATEWAY_OIDC_JWKS_URI:cjae-gateway-oidc-jwks-uri&quot; \
    &quot;GATEWAY_OIDC_AUDIENCE:cjae-gateway-oidc-audience&quot; \
    &quot;GATEWAY_OIDC_TENANT_CLAIM:cjae-gateway-oidc-tenant-claim&quot; \
    &quot;GATEWAY_OIDC_FIXED_SCOPES:cjae-gateway-oidc-fixed-scopes&quot; \
    &quot;GCP_WIF_PROVIDER_AUDIENCE:cjae-wif-provider-audience&quot; \
    &quot;GCP_KMS_KEY_RESOURCE:cjae-kms-key-resource&quot; \
    &quot;GCP_CONTEXT_KEY_ID:cjae-context-key-id&quot; \
    &quot;GCP_CONTEXT_DEK_CIPHERTEXT_BASE64:cjae-context-dek-ciphertext&quot;; do
    env_key=&quot;${mapping%%:*}&quot;
    metadata_key=&quot;${mapping#*:}&quot;
    value=&quot;$(metadata_value &quot;instance/attributes/${metadata_key}&quot;)&quot;
    if [[ -z &quot;${value}&quot; || &quot;${value}&quot; == *$&apos;\n&apos;* ]]; then
      echo &quot;Required instance metadata ${metadata_key} is missing or invalid.&quot; &gt;&amp;2
      exit 1
    fi
    printf &apos;%s=%s\n&apos; &quot;${env_key}&quot; &quot;${value}&quot; &gt;&gt; &quot;${GATEWAY_ENV_FILE}&quot;
  done
  printf &apos;%s\n&apos; \
    &quot;GCP_GCA_AUDIENCE=https://cjae-worker.cjae-confidential-engine&quot; \
    &quot;GCP_CVM_HARDWARE_MODEL=AMD_SEV&quot; \
    &quot;GCP_CVM_ALLOWED_PROJECTS=cjae-confidential-engine&quot; \
    &quot;GCP_CVM_ALLOWED_ZONES=us-central1-c&quot; \
    &quot;GCP_CVM_ALLOWED_INSTANCES=cjae-confidential-worker&quot; \
    &quot;GCP_GOTPM_PATH=/opt/cjae/bin/gotpm&quot; \
    &quot;GCP_TPM_EVENT_LOG_PATH=/opt/cjae/tpm/event.log&quot; &gt;&gt; &quot;${GATEWAY_ENV_FILE}&quot;
}

if [[ ! -c &quot;${TPM_DEVICE}&quot; ]]; then
  TPM_DEVICE=&quot;/dev/tpm0&quot;
  if [[ ! -c &quot;${TPM_DEVICE}&quot; ]]; then
    echo &quot;Required GCE vTPM device is unavailable.&quot; &gt;&amp;2
    exit 1
  fi
fi

write_gateway_environment

for required_key in \
  GATEWAY_OIDC_ISSUER \
  GATEWAY_OIDC_JWKS_URI \
  GATEWAY_OIDC_AUDIENCE \
  GATEWAY_OIDC_TENANT_CLAIM \
  GATEWAY_OIDC_FIXED_SCOPES \
  GCP_WIF_PROVIDER_AUDIENCE \
  GCP_KMS_KEY_RESOURCE \
  GCP_CONTEXT_KEY_ID \
  GCP_CONTEXT_DEK_CIPHERTEXT_BASE64; do
  if ! grep -q &quot;^${required_key}=.&quot; &quot;${GATEWAY_ENV_FILE}&quot;; then
    echo &quot;Required gateway setting ${required_key} is missing or empty in ${GATEWAY_ENV_FILE}&quot; &gt;&amp;2
    exit 1
  fi
done

export HOME=/home/appuser
docker-credential-gcr configure-docker --registries &quot;${REGISTRY}&quot;
docker pull &quot;${IMAGE}&quot;

project_id=&quot;$(metadata_value project/project-id)&quot;
zone=&quot;$(metadata_value instance/zone)&quot;
zone=&quot;${zone##*/}&quot;
instance_name=&quot;$(metadata_value instance/name)&quot;

install -d -m 0755 /run/cjae
if [[ ! -r /sys/kernel/security/tpm0/binary_bios_measurements ]]; then
  echo &quot;Required GCE TPM event log is unavailable.&quot; &gt;&amp;2
  exit 1
fi
install -m 0444 /sys/kernel/security/tpm0/binary_bios_measurements &quot;${TPM_EVENT_LOG}&quot;
chown root:65532 &quot;${TPM_DEVICE}&quot;
chmod 0660 &quot;${TPM_DEVICE}&quot;

docker rm --force cjae-worker-enclave &gt;/dev/null 2&gt;&amp;1 || true

docker run --detach \
  --name=cjae-worker-enclave \
  --restart=always \
  --device=&quot;${TPM_DEVICE}:${TPM_DEVICE}:rw&quot; \
  --read-only \
  --mount=&quot;type=bind,src=${TPM_EVENT_LOG},dst=/opt/cjae/tpm/event.log,readonly&quot; \
  --tmpfs /tmp:rw,noexec,nosuid,size=128m \
  --tmpfs /opt/cjae/logs:rw,noexec,nosuid,size=32m,uid=65532,gid=65532,mode=0750 \
  --publish 127.0.0.1:8000:8000 \
  --env-file &quot;${GATEWAY_ENV_FILE}&quot; \
  --env SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=gcp-cvm,gateway-oidc,onnx-pii \
  --env CONFIDENTIAL_GATEWAY_ENABLED=true \
  --env GCP_GCA_AUDIENCE=https://cjae-worker.cjae-confidential-engine \
  --env GCP_CVM_HARDWARE_MODEL=AMD_SEV \
  --env GCP_CVM_ALLOWED_PROJECTS=&quot;${project_id}&quot; \
  --env GCP_CVM_ALLOWED_ZONES=&quot;${zone}&quot; \
  --env GCP_CVM_ALLOWED_INSTANCES=&quot;${instance_name}&quot; \
  --env GCP_GOTPM_PATH=/opt/cjae/bin/gotpm \
  &quot;${IMAGE}&quot;</file><file path="java/opentron-java/backend/pom.xml">&lt;?xml version=&quot;1.0&quot; encoding=&quot;UTF-8&quot;?&gt;

&lt;/project&gt;</file><file path="java/opentron-java/backend/README.md">Opentron Java backend

This is a minimal Spring Boot application that implements a small subset of
the OpenTron OpenAI-compatible API used by the frontend. It exposes:

- `GET /v1/models` — returns available models
- `POST /v1/chat/completions` — returns a simple completion (echo of last user message)

Build and run:

```bash
cd java/opentron-java
mvn -pl backend -am package
java --enable-preview --enable-native-access=ALL-UNNAMED -jar backend/target/opentron-java-backend-0.1.0-exec.jar
```

The backend uses Java 21&apos;s preview Foreign Function &amp; Memory API. Backend tests
are launched with preview enabled by Maven; other Java 21 runtime launches must
also pass `--enable-preview`. Azure FFM downcalls additionally require
`--enable-native-access=ALL-UNNAMED`.

The server listens on port `8000` by default so it is a drop-in replacement for
the existing Python backend used by the frontend.

## GraalVM JVM confidential worker image

The confidential container runs the executable Spring Boot JAR on GraalVM
Java 21. This avoids the ONNX Runtime native-image startup crash observed with
the native executable while preserving regular JVM JNI loading:

```bash
docker build \
  --build-arg CJAE_ONNX_PII_MODEL_SHA256=&lt;reviewed-sha256&gt; \
  -f java/opentron-java/backend/Dockerfile.confidential \
  -t cjae-worker:latest .
```

The Dockerfile builds the standalone `confidential-worker` Maven project, not
the full backend reactor. Its compiler includes only the confidential
attestation, gateway, native-memory, and ONNX packages plus the worker entry
point. The worker has its own dependency list and configuration resources;
backend agents, connectors, persistence, and Ollama model-preloading code are
not packaged. To build it outside Docker:

```bash
mvn -f java/opentron-java/confidential-worker/pom.xml clean package
```

The image verifies the ONNX model digest and packages the Linux x86_64 ONNX
Runtime libraries alongside the JAR. It launches Java with Java 21 preview
FFM and native-access options, listens on port `8000`, and runs as an
unprivileged user. Provide the approved model and tokenizer files as
`deploy/models/pii_ner_v1.onnx`, `deploy/models/vocab.txt`, and
`deploy/models/special_tokens_map.json`.

The Dockerfile-specific ignore file keeps unrelated application source out
of the build context. The GraalVM JVM image uses glibc; Alpine/musl is not a compatible
runtime base. The model digest does not authenticate the tokenizer files, so
supply all three files from the same trusted, immutable model release. The
separate native-image Maven profile remains available, but is not used for
this ONNX-enabled deployment image. ONNX Runtime is directed to the
read-only `/opt/cjae/lib` directory for JNI loading so its shared objects do
not need to be extracted to the container&apos;s no-exec `/tmp`. The NER adapter
accepts `input_ids` and `attention_mask`, with optional BERT
`token_type_ids` (zero-filled for single-sequence inputs).

## Confidential Gateway prototype (local and untrusted)

The initial sanitize/desanitize API is an explicitly untrusted local
prototype. It uses limited Java rules for email addresses and common North
American phone numbers, plus a configured local-development AES-GCM context
key. It does not perform Azure attestation, release hardware-gated keys,
provide comprehensive PII detection, or make the JVM confidential. Do not
send real sensitive data or expose these endpoints to a network.

The `/sanitize` and `/sanitize/batch` routes parse JSON from the servlet input
stream and decode prompt text directly into bounded, confined Java 21 native
memory. Batch text shares one native allocation through zero-copy slices.
Governor permits are acquired before parsing, and the parser enforces body,
segment-count, and character limits. This avoids creating a whole prompt
`String` at the HTTP binding boundary. `ConfidentialSegment.fromUtf8` also
encodes direct callers&apos; input strings into native memory without an
intermediate heap `byte[]`.

This is application-level copy reduction, not heap elimination or a
confidentiality boundary. Matched PII is retained as native-memory slices;
the secure-context codec serializes and encrypts those slices using direct
buffers and decrypts back into native memory. The `/desanitize` and
`/desanitize/batch` routes also parse model responses into native segments
instead of binding them as heap strings, and stream restored JSON from native
segments through a bounded scratch buffer that is zeroed after writes.
Sanitized HTTP responses are now assembled and JSON-encoded from native
segments without materializing the complete sanitized prompt as a Java
`String`; existing service methods that explicitly return string result
records remain available to internal callers. Opaque replacement tokens and
entity/WordPiece ID and offset metadata still use ordinary Java objects.
ONNX Unicode normalization still creates transient heap strings, although
WordPiece matching no longer creates a candidate string for every vocabulary
probe. Servlet containers, the JVM, and native libraries may also make
internal copies. The legacy service overloads that accept `String` remain
available to internal callers.

For deployments handling real sensitive data, disable process core dumps and
JVM heap dumps, restrict diagnostic/debug access, and either disable swap or
use encrypted ephemeral swap. For example, configure the service manager with
`LimitCORE=0` and launch the JVM with `-XX:-HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError`.
These controls reduce persistence risk; they do not erase JVM/provider copies
or protect against privileged memory inspection.

Secure-context payloads use version `v2` with a binary encoding. Contexts
created by the prior `v1` JSON format are not accepted by this version; allow
the configured context lifetime to expire before deployment or invalidate
outstanding contexts as part of rollout.

The confidential gateway limits active sanitize/desanitize work with a fair
semaphore governor. It defaults to 64 operations per process and can be
changed with `CONFIDENTIAL_GATEWAY_MAX_CONCURRENT_OPERATIONS`; the streaming
sanitize routes hold a permit for parsing and processing, while virtual
threads waiting for permits park rather than consuming a platform thread.
This governor does not itself switch the servlet server to virtual request
threads.

### ONNX PII model inference

The optional `onnx-pii` profile verifies a deployment-mounted model by its
pinned SHA-256, opens an ONNX Runtime session, and supplies a BERT WordPiece
tokenizer for token-classification inference. When this profile is active, its
`PiiDetector` bean replaces the prototype rule detector for the configured
gateway; without it, other profiles continue to use the prototype detector.
Inputs are `input_ids` and `attention_mask` as INT64
`[1, sequence_length]`; output is FLOAT32 logits shaped
`[1, sequence_length, num_labels]`. BIO predictions are mapped back to original
Java-string offsets. Long inputs are evaluated with overlapping windows.

The model label IDs must be configured as JSON using contiguous integer keys
starting at zero and `O`/`B-TYPE`/`I-TYPE` labels. The configured
`dslim/bert-base-NER` model mapping is
`{&quot;0&quot;:&quot;O&quot;,&quot;1&quot;:&quot;B-MISC&quot;,&quot;2&quot;:&quot;I-MISC&quot;,&quot;3&quot;:&quot;B-PER&quot;,&quot;4&quot;:&quot;I-PER&quot;,&quot;5&quot;:&quot;B-ORG&quot;,&quot;6&quot;:&quot;I-ORG&quot;,&quot;7&quot;:&quot;B-LOC&quot;,&quot;8&quot;:&quot;I-LOC&quot;}`.
Mount the model, `vocab.txt`, and `special_tokens_map.json` at the configured
paths. The tokenizer supports BERT&apos;s basic token splitting and greedy WordPiece
segmentation; it is not a replacement for model-specific normalization
options, added tokens, or custom preprocessing. Check these details against
the approved model before deployment. The ONNX file is SHA-256 pinned;
vocabulary and special-token files are not independently hash-pinned by this
profile, so mount them from the same trusted, immutable deployment artifact.

The artifact SHA-256 is streamed in 1 MiB chunks directly into
`ConfidentialSegment` native memory. `FileChannel` reads into a direct view of
that segment, `MessageDigest` consumes its `ByteBuffer` view, and each chunk is
zeroed before reuse. The full model is not copied into heap or native memory.
Activate with `onnx-pii` only after mounting an approved model at
`/opt/cjae/models/pii_ner_v1.onnx` and setting
`CJAE_ONNX_PII_MODEL_SHA256` to its reviewed digest.

The configured checkpoint is
`963039b81eec5b33e23d84826ccdf1e8f8ada776f320e692113034cfae384617`, matching
the deployment ONNX file currently staged in `deploy/models`. Empty model
files are rejected, and verification must pass against the actual read-only
deployment artifact before enabling the profile. Do not treat a configured
digest alone as model approval.
Inference uses ONNX Runtime&apos;s platform-native library. Direct native input
buffers for token IDs and attention masks are cleared after each run; Java
strings and tokenizer/output metadata are still managed-heap objects, so this
is not end-to-end off-heap processing. The actual approved model/tokenizer
combination still requires separate validation. Deploy the model from an
immutable, read-only mount and use a
self-contained ONNX file; the configured digest currently pins the `.onnx`
file only and does not authenticate external tensor-data sidecars.

#### Deployment validation procedure

Run these checks on the immutable target image, not on a workstation, and keep
the outputs as non-secret deployment evidence. Do not print credentials,
attestation tokens, secure contexts, or released keys.

1. Confirm the mounted model digest and require an exact match before startup:

   ```bash
   MODEL=/opt/cjae/models/pii_ner_v1.onnx
   EXPECTED=963039b81eec5b33e23d84826ccdf1e8f8ada776f320e692113034cfae384617
   test -r &quot;$MODEL&quot;
   ACTUAL=$(sha256sum &quot;$MODEL&quot; | cut -d &apos; &apos; -f 1)
   test &quot;$ACTUAL&quot; = &quot;$EXPECTED&quot;
   ```

2. On an Ubuntu Linux build host with the documented Microsoft guest
   attestation dependencies installed, compile the C ABI bridge and verify
   both exported symbols using the CMake/`nm` commands in the Azure build
   section below. The CI build checks compilation and exports; it does not
   constitute a TEE test.
3. On an Azure SEV-SNP CVM, install the bridge and `libazguestattestation`
   runtime library, set the Azure and ONNX profile properties from the
   deployment&apos;s approved MAA/Key Vault configuration, and start the Java 21
   service with profiles `azure-cvm,gateway-oidc,onnx-pii`. The current
   Validate the documented Java 21 JVM launch path, including ONNX Runtime
   initialization, on the target CVM before enabling production traffic.
4. Send an authenticated sanitize request containing a controlled test email
   and phone number. Require a successful fresh attestation/key-release path,
   masked output for both entities, and successful desanitization back to the
   exact test input. Repeat with an invalid model digest and disallowed MAA
   measurement/policy and require startup or the operation to fail closed.
5. Record the image/build identifiers, model SHA-256, non-secret policy
   identifiers, test case outcomes, and timestamps. Never include bearer
   tokens, JWTs, ciphertext/plaintext DEKs, user prompts, or secure contexts.

This procedure is a test plan, not a claim of execution. Live model inference
requires the deployment-mounted model and tokenizer package; hardware key
release requires an Azure CVM, MAA/Key Vault configuration, and the native
guest libraries. The equivalent AWS Nitro live procedure additionally needs
deployment-supplied NSM evidence and a parent-vsock proxy, as noted in the
AWS adapter status below.

Enable the API only in the `local-untrusted` Spring profile with
`CONFIDENTIAL_GATEWAY_ENABLED=true`. Configure a random bearer token (at least
32 characters) and a Base64-encoded 32-byte AES key. In PowerShell:

```powershell
$tokenBytes = [byte[]]::new(32)
$keyBytes = [byte[]]::new(32)
$rng = [System.Security.Cryptography.RandomNumberGenerator]::Create()
$rng.GetBytes($tokenBytes)
$rng.GetBytes($keyBytes)
$rng.Dispose()
$env:CONFIDENTIAL_GATEWAY_API_TOKEN = ([BitConverter]::ToString($tokenBytes)).Replace(&quot;-&quot;, &quot;&quot;).ToLowerInvariant()
$env:CONFIDENTIAL_GATEWAY_CONTEXT_KEY = [Convert]::ToBase64String($keyBytes)
$env:CONFIDENTIAL_GATEWAY_TENANT_ID = &quot;local-development&quot;
$env:CONFIDENTIAL_GATEWAY_ENABLED = &quot;true&quot;
$env:SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE = &quot;local-untrusted&quot;

mvn spring-boot:run &quot;-Dspring-boot.run.jvmArguments=--enable-preview --enable-native-access=ALL-UNNAMED&quot; &quot;-Dspring-boot.run.arguments=--server.address=127.0.0.1&quot;
```

The API is bound to loopback in this example. Use the bearer token in the
`Authorization` header:

- `POST /v1/confidential/sanitize` with `{&quot;text&quot;:&quot;Contact ada@example.com&quot;}`
- `POST /v1/confidential/desanitize` with
  `{&quot;modelResponse&quot;:&quot;...&quot;,&quot;secureContext&quot;:&quot;...&quot;}`
- `POST /v1/confidential/sanitize/batch` with
  `{&quot;segments&quot;:[&quot;Contact ada@example.com&quot;,&quot;Call 212-555-0199&quot;]}`
- `POST /v1/confidential/desanitize/batch` with
  `{&quot;modelResponses&quot;:[&quot;...&quot;,&quot;...&quot;],&quot;secureContext&quot;:&quot;...&quot;}`

Responses use `Cache-Control: no-store`. Contexts are tenant-bound, expire
after ten minutes by default, and can only be opened while the configured key
is available. Batch requests support 1-100 segments with a 5 MB aggregate text
limit and use one secure context for the batch. This prototype supports a
single configured tenant; the tenant identifier is not a substitute for
production identity or authorization.

The API authenticates the bearer credential through a pluggable
`GatewayCredentialAuthenticator` and derives tenant identity and operation
scopes from the resulting `GatewayPrincipal`; tenant IDs in request bodies
are ignored. The local profile uses a static credential and grants both
gateway scopes to its one configured development tenant. It also applies
process-local fixed-window request and character quotas (60 requests and
5,000,000 characters per minute by default, with a 10,000-tenant state cap).
These are development safeguards, not distributed enforcement.

An OIDC/JWT authentication adapter is available under the `gateway-oidc`
Spring profile. Configure `confidential.gateway.oidc.issuer`,
`confidential.gateway.oidc.jwks-uri`, and
`confidential.gateway.oidc.audience`; optionally configure
`confidential.gateway.oidc.tenant-claim` (default `tenant_id`) and
`confidential.gateway.oidc.scopes-claim` (default `scope`). Issuer and JWKS
URLs must be HTTPS. The decoder validates the issuer, token time claims, and
signature from the configured JWKS; the authenticator additionally requires
the configured audience and a tenant claim, and only grants the recognized
`gateway:sanitize` and `gateway:desanitize` scopes from the validated scope
claim. The tenant claim mapping must be agreed with the identity provider;
never accept tenant or scopes from request data.

The `gateway-oidc` profile alone only creates the OIDC decoder and
authenticator. The Azure-backed API can be enabled by activating both
`azure-cvm` and `gateway-oidc` and setting
`confidential.gateway.enabled=true`; this wires OIDC authorization to the
Gateway endpoints and uses the Azure-attested key provider for secure
contexts. The local static bearer credential is not used on that profile
combination. This is an integration path for target-environment validation,
not a production-ready service: unless the `onnx-pii` profile and its approved
model package are also configured, the PII detector is still the prototype
rules detector. Quotas remain process-local, and credential
provisioning/rotation and the API/worker trust boundary are not implemented.

The Gateway API remains disabled unless a supported profile and explicit
enable flag are active. Production support requires target-environment
validation of the selected provider integration and a verified
application-measurement provenance chain before any confidential-runtime
claim is made.

### Current status and external setup required

The code and offline tests provide implementation foundations, not a
production-ready confidential service. The local endpoints require
`local-untrusted`; the OIDC-protected Azure or GCP API requires the matching
provider profile and `gateway-oidc`, plus the explicit
`confidential.gateway.enabled=true` switch. Activating `gateway-oidc` alone
does not expose the Gateway API. Do not combine the OIDC profile with
`local-untrusted` and assume that the API is using OIDC; that profile uses the
local static-token authenticator.

| Area | Implemented now | Still required |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Local Gateway | Single-process sanitize/desanitize API, tenant-bound contexts, static local bearer, request/character quotas | Comprehensive PII detection, production auth wiring, distributed quotas, production request limits and worker boundary |
| OIDC/JWT | HTTPS JWKS decoder, claim-validation adapter, and opt-in wiring to Azure/GCP Gateway APIs with offline tests | Identity-provider registration/configuration, agreed tenant/scope claims, rotation/provisioning, distributed quotas and end-to-end issuer tests |
| Azure attestation and key release | Java MAA JWT verifier, guest-attestation Panama FFM adapter, Key Vault release client and key unwrap code, with synthetic/offline tests | Linux native build confirmation and a real Azure AMD SEV-SNP VM test of fresh evidence, MAA policy/claims, Key Vault release and TPM unwrap |
| Application provenance | Offline Ed25519 manifest/artifact/SBOM verifier | Measured boot/launcher and a demonstrated cryptographic binding from approved application artifacts to attestation and key release |
| Deployment and integrations | Standalone Java client JAR for sanitize/desanitize, non-streaming completion, and role-preserving conversation history; `@ConfidentialAI` verification aspect | Isolated worker deployment, framework-specific adapters for retrieval/tool boundaries and streaming, and operational/security review |
| GCP Confidential VM | GCA JWT verifier, nonce/VM policy checks, checksum-pinned `gotpm` token-source integration, WIF-to-KMS key provider, runtime verifier, and opt-in OIDC Gateway wiring with offline tests | Real GCP validation, WIF provider/IAM/KMS provisioning and policy review, measured application provenance, OIDC identity-provider setup, and production PII/quota/worker controls |
| AWS Nitro Enclaves | Enclave-side nonce/public-key recipient key-release service, PCR-policy descriptor and profile-based key-provider/runtime-verifier wiring with offline tests | Deployment-supplied NSM evidence and parent-vsock transport beans (required for startup), enclave worker image/deployment, IAM/KMS key-policy provisioning and verification, and live AWS enclave tests |

#### Integrating the Java client JAR

`org.opentron:opentron-confidential-gateway-client:0.1.0` is a standalone
Java 21 client artifact. It depends on Jackson Databind and the JDK HTTP client,
not the Gateway&apos;s Spring, database, or cloud-provider runtime dependencies.
The Gateway server and this client are separate deployment components: client
applications need a running Gateway endpoint and a bearer credential with the
required scopes.

Build and install the artifact into the local Maven repository from the
repository root:

```powershell
mvn -f java/opentron-java/pom.xml -pl gateway-client -am install
```

Then add the dependency to the integrating application&apos;s `pom.xml`:

```xml
&lt;dependency&gt;
  &lt;groupId&gt;org.opentron&lt;/groupId&gt;
  &lt;artifactId&gt;opentron-confidential-gateway-client&lt;/artifactId&gt;
  &lt;version&gt;0.1.0&lt;/version&gt;
&lt;/dependency&gt;
```

Use a TLS Gateway endpoint outside loopback development. The conversation
helper sanitizes the content of every role together under one secure context,
passes only sanitized messages to the supplied model callback, then restores
the model response before returning it to the application:

```java
var gateway = new ConfidentialGatewayClient(
    URI.create(&quot;https://gateway.example.com&quot;),
    System.getenv(&quot;CONFIDENTIAL_GATEWAY_API_TOKEN&quot;),
    HttpClient.newHttpClient(),
    new ObjectMapper(),
    Duration.ofSeconds(30));
var conversation = new ConfidentialConversationClient(gateway);
var answer = conversation.complete(
    List.of(
        new ConfidentialConversationClient.Message(&quot;system&quot;, systemPrompt),
        new ConfidentialConversationClient.Message(&quot;user&quot;, userPrompt)),
    sanitizedMessages -&gt; model.complete(sanitizedMessages));
```

The model adapter must use only the supplied sanitized content when constructing
its provider request; do not close over or forward the original prompt,
retrieval chunks, or tool arguments. The helper handles non-streaming message
content only; tool-call metadata and streaming restoration require a
framework-specific integration and are not implemented. Treat secure contexts
as sensitive bearer data, do not log or persist them, and ensure all sensitive
prompt components are included in the same sanitize call. The local Gateway
prototype is not suitable for real sensitive data.

#### Identity-provider setup (when an OIDC account is available)

An identity-provider tenant/account is needed to validate the adapter against
real signed tokens. Configure an API/resource audience for the Gateway and
issue access tokens whose `iss`, `aud`, expiry and optional `nbf` claims match
the configured values. Define a trusted tenant claim (default `tenant_id`)
and a scopes claim (default `scope`) that emits `gateway:sanitize` and/or
`gateway:desanitize`. The application registering/obtaining tokens must be
authorized for that resource and scopes. To enable the Azure-backed API,
activate both `azure-cvm` and `gateway-oidc`, set
`confidential.gateway.enabled=true`, and configure:

```text
SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=azure-cvm,gateway-oidc
confidential.gateway.enabled=true
confidential.gateway.oidc.issuer=https://&lt;identity-provider&gt;/&lt;tenant-or-realm&gt;
confidential.gateway.oidc.jwks-uri=https://&lt;identity-provider&gt;/&lt;jwks-endpoint&gt;
confidential.gateway.oidc.audience=&lt;registered-gateway-api-audience&gt;
confidential.gateway.oidc.tenant-claim=tenant_id
confidential.gateway.oidc.scopes-claim=scope
```

Use the issuer and JWKS URL published by the chosen provider, not guessed
endpoints. Do not put a client secret in the Gateway: this adapter validates
bearer access tokens and does not acquire tokens. The Azure and GCP API
configurations wire this authenticator into the HTTP API; verify key rotation,
invalid signatures, wrong issuer/audience, expiry, tenant mapping, and scope
denial with the selected provider before deployment. Azure&apos;s configuration
properties and target VM prerequisites below must also be supplied. For GCP,
use the provider configuration below. The image includes a checksum-pinned
Google `gotpm` utility as its nonce-bound GCA token source; the VM must expose
its vTPM device and have the documented GCA API permissions and OAuth scope.
These opt-in profiles still use the prototype PII detector and in-memory
quotas; do not expose them to production traffic or process real sensitive
data.

#### Where to configure external account/resource values

Spring automatically loads the profile property files
`application-gateway-oidc.properties`, `application-azure-cvm.properties`,
`application-gcp-cvm.properties`, and `application-aws-nitro.properties`.
They read environment variables rather than containing account-specific
values or credentials. Set the variables in the cloud workload environment,
deployment platform, or secret/configuration manager. For local testing,
PowerShell uses `$env:NAME=&quot;value&quot;`; do not commit populated property files,
tokens, private keys, or secret values.

The property prefixes for provider values and their sources are:

| Provider/profile | Account/resource settings | Credential source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| OIDC (`gateway-oidc`) | Issuer, JWKS URI, audience, tenant claim and scopes claim | Public identity-provider metadata/JWKS; clients send short-lived access tokens; Gateway has no client secret |
| Azure (`azure-cvm`) | MAA OpenID configuration and endpoint URIs, issuer/audience, approved SNP launch measurements and policy hash; Key Vault URI/key name/version/context key ID | Azure managed identity from the guest; optional user-assigned identity client ID |
| GCP (`gcp-cvm`) | GCA audience, hardware model, allowed project/zone/instance; canonical WIF provider audience; KMS CryptoKey resource, context key ID and encrypted DEK ciphertext | The packaged `gotpm` utility obtains nonce-bound GCA evidence through the vTPM; WIF exchanges verified GCA JWT for short-lived Google credentials |
| AWS (`aws-nitro`) | KMS key ARN/ID, encrypted DEK ciphertext, context key ID, EIF ImageSha384 and required PCR values | Parent-instance role/credential provider in the vsock proxy; enclave-side NSM and vsock transport implementations are deployment supplied |

The actual account values should be set as the corresponding environment
variables referenced in the profile files. For example, `AZURE_KEY_VAULT_URI`
is the vault URI from the Azure account, `GCP_KMS_KEY_RESOURCE` is the
fully-qualified CryptoKey resource, and `AWS_NITRO_KMS_KEY_ID` is the KMS key
ARN/ID. All measurements and PCR values must be collected from the exact
approved images and enforced by the relevant cloud-side policy; never copy
example measurements into production.

#### Azure account and target-environment setup

The Azure path cannot be fully validated with generated test tokens or an
ordinary development machine. The following resources and permissions are
needed for the real test:

1. An Azure subscription with permission to deploy and operate an AMD SEV-SNP
   Confidential VM in a supported region/size, and access to the guest image
   and Linux Microsoft guest-attestation package used by the build.
2. An Azure Attestation resource/provider configured for the target VM type.
   Record its actual OpenID configuration URL, expected issuer, policy
   version/hash, and the claims emitted by the guest. Derive the approved
   launch-measurement allowlist and minimum security-version values from the
   intended, repeatable image deployment.
3. An Azure Key Vault or Managed HSM with a key configured for Secure Key
   Release and a release policy that independently enforces the intended
   attestation evidence. Record the vault URI, key name/version, and context
   key identifier. Configure the VM&apos;s managed identity and grant only the
   permissions required to request that key&apos;s release; use the optional
   managed-identity client ID only for a user-assigned identity.
4. Network/DNS and host-image setup allowing the guest to reach the required
   Azure Attestation, Key Vault, and managed-identity endpoints, while
   keeping the Gateway&apos;s application/model traffic within the approved
   network policy. Install the native bridge and its runtime dependencies in
   the target image.
5. Run an end-to-end test on that VM: obtain fresh nonce-bound guest evidence,
   inspect the resulting MAA token and claims, exercise allow and deny policy
   cases, request Secure Key Release, unwrap the released key through the
   guest TPM, and confirm expiry/failure behavior. Save non-secret test
   results, policy/version identifiers, and measurements as deployment
   evidence; never commit credentials or released keys.

The `confidential.azure.*` property names are listed below. Their values must
come from the actual Azure resources and target evidence, not example or
synthetic values. Azure subscription access, resource creation, VM deployment,
managed-identity authorization, the real native build, TPM behavior, and the
end-to-end release flow remain external validation gates; none is proven by
the offline unit tests.

## Azure AMD SEV-SNP integration

The `azure-cvm` Spring profile includes the Java verifier and Key Vault
Secure Key Release client plus a Project Panama FFM binding to a small C ABI
bridge around Microsoft&apos;s Linux
`azguestattestation` C++ client. The bridge binds a fresh 256-bit nonce into
the guest-attestation request, invokes TPM-backed `Attest`, extracts the
hybrid-encrypted key from the Key Vault release JWS, TPM-unwraps the transfer
key with RSA-OAEP-SHA256, and AES-256-KWP unwraps the released context key.
Sensitive native key buffers are cleared before being returned/freed. The
native bridge serializes access to Microsoft&apos;s singleton client.

The Java verifier checks RS256 JWT signatures against configured Azure
Attestation OpenID metadata/JWKS, issuer and optional audience, token times,
the nonce in the documented `x-ms-runtime.client-payload` shape, Azure CVM
and nested SEV-SNP claim types, Azure compliance status, Secure Boot/vTPM,
TPM ephemeral encryption-key presence, disabled SNP debugging, VMPL 0, the
configured policy hash, an allowlisted launch measurement, and configured
minimum SNP security versions. This follows the actual nested claim layout
produced by Microsoft&apos;s Linux client sample.

### Build the native bridge

Build and run the bridge on Linux x86-64 with JDK 21 and the Microsoft guest
attestation package installed. The upstream sample currently installs package
version `azguestattestation1_1.0.5_amd64.deb`; use a supported package version
for the target image. Install CMake, a C++17 compiler, OpenSSL
development files, and nlohmann-json development files, then build:

```bash
sudo apt-get update
sudo apt-get install -y build-essential cmake openjdk-21-jdk-headless \
  libssl-dev nlohmann-json3-dev
wget https://packages.microsoft.com/repos/azurecore/pool/main/a/azguestattestation1/azguestattestation1_1.0.5_amd64.deb
sudo dpkg -i azguestattestation1_1.0.5_amd64.deb

cmake -S src/main/native/azure-attestation \
  -B target/native/azure-attestation
cmake --build target/native/azure-attestation --config Release
nm -D --defined-only target/native/azure-attestation/libopentron_azure_attestation.so \
  | grep -E &apos;opentron_azure_(attest|unwrap_key)&apos;
```

Run the commands above from `java/opentron-java/backend` within the repository.
Ensure `libopentron_azure_attestation.so` is on `java.library.path` and the
Microsoft `libazguestattestation` runtime library is available to the dynamic
linker. The native library uses the Microsoft API and its TPM-backed
decryption; it is not a hardware simulator. Windows developer machines cannot
run this Linux CVM bridge. The repository&apos;s
`Azure Guest Attestation Native Build` workflow compiles the bridge on Ubuntu
for relevant pull requests and pushes; a passing CI build proves source/API
and linker compatibility only, not CVM execution or TPM behavior.

Required settings:

```text
confidential.azure.attestation.openid-configuration-uri
confidential.azure.attestation.endpoint-uri
confidential.azure.attestation.issuer
confidential.azure.attestation.audience (optional; the documented CVM token omits aud)
confidential.azure.attestation.approved-launch-measurements
confidential.azure.attestation.policy-hash
confidential.azure.attestation.minimum-security-versions (optional; claim=value pairs)
confidential.azure.key-vault.uri
confidential.azure.key-vault.key-name
confidential.azure.key-vault.key-version
confidential.azure.key-vault.context-key-id
confidential.azure.key-vault.managed-identity-client-id (optional)
```

Use only values read from the actual target MAA token and the release policy
deployed for that target. Pin the expected MAA issuer and, if present, audience.
The Key Vault key must be configured for Secure Key Release with a policy that
independently restricts allowed evidence. Configure a non-empty audience only
if the deployed MAA token actually contains one; the documented CVM Linux
sample emits `x-ms-attestation-type=azurevm` and nests `sevsnpvm` under
`x-ms-isolation-tee`.

The native bridge has not been built or run in this environment. A Linux CI
build is configured, but its result must be observed before claiming native
compilation. TPM calls and the full release path still require validation on
an Azure Confidential VM. The `launchmeasurement` claim is not presumed to
measure the Gateway JAR or its dependencies; no application binary provenance
guarantee is currently implemented. The AES key is ultimately materialized
in the JVM guest and is not protected from compromised code inside that
guest.

### Google Cloud Confidential VM adapter

The `gcp-cvm` profile provides a Java adapter that validates a fresh
audience/nonce-bound token against Google&apos;s pinned GCA signing-key endpoint,
then checks issuer, audience, EAT profile, token times, nonce, Secure Boot,
selected AMD SEV or Intel TDX hardware model, and allowlisted GCE
project/zone/instance identity. Google documents GCA token claims and a
launcher-mediated attestation flow, but the specific metadata URL previously
considered for obtaining a GCA token is not documented by the reviewed
sources. The adapter therefore requires a deployment-supplied
`GcpAttestationTokenSource`; it does not ship or claim a default token-fetching
endpoint. This source must obtain the signed token through an approved GCA
flow and must bind the supplied fresh challenge as the GCA nonce. The verifier
supports the documented `eat_nonce` string or array claim and requires the
challenge to be present. It does not assert that GCA claims measure the
Gateway JAR.

For key access, the adapter exchanges the verified GCA JWT through Google&apos;s
Security Token Service using a Workload Identity Federation provider, then
calls Cloud KMS `decrypt` for a previously encrypted 256-bit context key. The
key is not embedded in configuration as plaintext. To use the Gateway API,
activate `gcp-cvm,gateway-oidc` with
`confidential.gateway.enabled=true`; do not activate Azure and GCP provider
profiles together. The GCP API configuration currently shares the local
prototype PII detector and process-local quota implementation.

Required application properties:

```text
confidential.gcp.attestation.audience
confidential.gcp.attestation.hardware-model=AMD_SEV|INTEL_TDX
confidential.gcp.attestation.allowed-projects
confidential.gcp.attestation.allowed-zones
confidential.gcp.attestation.allowed-instances
confidential.gcp.attestation.gotpm-path=/opt/cjae/bin/gotpm
confidential.gcp.attestation.event-log-path=/opt/cjae/tpm/event.log
confidential.gcp.wif.audience
confidential.gcp.kms.key-resource
confidential.gcp.kms.context-key-id
confidential.gcp.kms.ciphertext-base64
```

External setup requires a GCP project and supported Confidential VM, enabled
Cloud APIs, Confidential Computing, Security Token Service and Cloud KMS APIs,
the `cloud-platform` OAuth scope, a service account with
`confidentialcomputing.challenges.create`,
`confidentialcomputing.challenges.verify`,
`confidentialcomputing.locations.get`, and
`confidentialcomputing.locations.list` permissions, an
IAM Workload Identity Pool/provider configured to trust the Google GCA issuer
and the exact attestation audience/claims, and a principal binding that grants
only the required KMS decrypt permission on the selected CryptoKey. The key
must contain a previously encrypted 32-byte DEK; supply its ciphertext in
Base64 form and protect it as configuration. Restrict the WIF provider with
attribute conditions for the expected hardware model, Secure Boot, project,
zone and instance. The exact GCA audience and claims must be confirmed on the
target VM/provider setup.
For classic AMD SEV, `gotpm` is invoked with the explicitly mounted vTPM event
log and without a `--tee-technology` hardware quote selector. Do not treat this
vTPM evidence as SEV-SNP attestation; the configured GCA verifier must accept
the resulting `GCP_AMD_SEV` hardware claims.

The deployed worker combines `gcp-cvm`, `onnx-pii`, and `embedded`; its H2
database is in-memory and is lost whenever the container restarts. Configure
Cloud SQL or another supported durable database before relying on persisted
application state. The runtime can start without WIF/KMS values while the
confidential Gateway API is disabled. Enabling
`confidential.gateway.enabled` requires the WIF/KMS properties and the
separate `gateway-oidc` issuer, JWKS URI, and audience. The code locally
validates the attestation JWT before token exchange; external WIF and KMS
configuration must independently enforce the intended trust policy. No GCP
enclave/worker boundary or application-to-measurement provenance mechanism is
included.

### AWS Nitro Enclaves adapter boundary

The `gateway.aws` package contains an enclave-side key-release service. It
creates a fresh nonce and RSA recipient key pair, asks an injected NSM source
for an attestation document bound to both, sends that document as AWS KMS
`Decrypt.Recipient`, and unwraps only KMS&apos;s `CiphertextForRecipient` response
inside the enclave-side service. It requires a 256-bit AES context key. The
PCR/image policy helper describes AWS KMS key-policy condition values for
`kms:RecipientAttestation:ImageSha384` (PCR0) and selected PCRs.

This is deliberately **not** wired as a normal Gateway HTTP provider:
Nitro Enclaves have no external networking and require an isolated enclave
worker plus a parent-instance vsock proxy to AWS KMS. The `aws-nitro` profile
now provides an AF_VSOCK transport to parent CID 3, port 8000 by default;
override with `AWS_NITRO_PARENT_VSOCK_CID` and
`AWS_NITRO_KMS_PROXY_VSOCK_PORT`. It sends a four-byte network-order frame
length followed by UTF-8 JSON:

```json
{&quot;version&quot;:1,&quot;operation&quot;:&quot;kms:Decrypt&quot;,&quot;request&quot;:{&quot;KeyId&quot;:&quot;...&quot;,&quot;CiphertextBlob&quot;:&quot;base64&quot;,&quot;Recipient&quot;:{&quot;AttestationDocument&quot;:&quot;base64&quot;,&quot;KeyEncryptionAlgorithm&quot;:&quot;RSAES_OAEP_SHA_256&quot;}}}
```

The proxy must return the same four-byte length framing and UTF-8 JSON of the
form `{&quot;version&quot;:1,&quot;response&quot;:{&quot;CiphertextForRecipient&quot;:&quot;base64&quot;}}`, execute
the AWS KMS request using parent-instance credentials, and never return
`Plaintext`. Socket read/write timeouts and frame-size bounds are enforced.
The Nitro enclave still requires a deployment-supplied NSM implementation and
an authenticated, least-privilege parent proxy; this transport does not
authenticate the proxy protocol by itself. The KMS condition helper does not
install or verify the remote key policy; KMS enforces only conditions that
administrators actually configure.

External setup, before this can run on AWS, includes an enclave-capable EC2
parent, an EIF image and measured PCR values, a Nitro Enclave with appropriate
CPU/memory allocation, a parent vsock proxy with AWS KMS credentials, an IAM
role with least-privilege decrypt access, and a KMS key policy restricting
`Recipient` requests to the approved image/PCR values. Test both allowed and
denied PCRs and prove that plaintext is returned only to the enclave. The
current implementation is a tested protocol boundary, not a deployable or
live-validated Nitro adapter.

### Application-measurement provenance plan (not implemented)

Treat the SNP `launchmeasurement` as a guest-launch assertion only. To make an
application identity claim, the deployment needs an independently verifiable
chain from an approved build to the running application:

1. Produce a reproducible, signed build manifest identifying the Gateway
   artifact digest, dependency/SBOM digests, source revision, build workflow,
   and builder identity. Verify the signature against a pinned release key.
2. Package the runtime into an immutable, signed boot image with Secure Boot
   enforcing the firmware, bootloader, kernel, and initramfs chain. Do not
   fetch or modify executable artifacts after the measured boot boundary.
3. Have a trusted, measured launcher verify the manifest and artifact
   digests before starting the Gateway. Extend an approved vTPM PCR with the
   manifest/artifact digest and create a TPM quote bound to the fresh
   attestation challenge.
4. Verify the quote, event log, PCR policy, and signed build provenance
   outside the guest. Release secrets only if the attestation path cryptographically
   binds that verified application identity to the guest evidence and nonce.

The existing Azure JWT verifier does not validate a TPM quote, event log, build
manifest, or application digest. The guest&apos;s self-reported
`x-ms-runtime.client-payload` is nonce-bound input, not independent proof of
which executable is running. Before implementing this plan, confirm that the
chosen Azure Attestation and Key Vault policy path can validate and enforce
the required quote/measurement binding; if it cannot, introduce a separate
verifier/key-release gate rather than treating a guest-supplied digest as
attested. This entire chain remains a design requirement until demonstrated
on the target deployment.

The offline tests sign synthetic tokens with generated local RSA keys. They
cover signature, nonce, issuer/audience, token expiry, the documented nested
CVM claim structure, SNP type, compliance status, secure boot/vTPM/debug
state, TPM key presence, measurement allowlist, policy hash, and minimum
security-version checks. They do not prove Azure claims, Azure policy
evaluation, VM measurements, Key Vault release, or guest-library decryption.
Do not describe the Gateway as hardware-confidential until those cases pass
in a real target Azure environment.

### PII detector baseline evaluation

Run the synthetic detector evaluation with:

```bash
mvn -Dtest=PiiDetectorEvaluationTest test
```

The report includes exact-span micro-precision/recall/F1, recall by labeled
entity category and language, p50/p95 per-document latency, and approximate
per-thread allocated bytes per detection. Allocation uses the JVM&apos;s
`ThreadMXBean` allocation counter where supported; it is not retained-memory
usage. Latency/allocation values are environment-specific and should be
collected on a controlled machine for comparisons.

This small, hand-labeled synthetic corpus is a smoke-test baseline, not a
representative evaluation dataset. It intentionally includes unsupported
entities and languages to show coverage gaps. Do not use these scores to
claim production detection quality. Before considering replacement detectors,
expand and independently review the corpus, benchmark each candidate against
the same spans, measure language/entity coverage and operational costs, and
review each model/library license and distribution obligations. No external
PII detector has been selected or licensed into this prototype.

### Signed build-manifest verifier (release-stage utility)

`AzureBuildProvenanceVerifier` verifies an Ed25519 signature over the exact
manifest bytes, then checks the SHA-256 of the named artifact and its adjacent
`&lt;artifact&gt;.sbom.json`. The version-1 manifest fields are:

```json
{
  &quot;schemaVersion&quot;: 1,
  &quot;artifact&quot;: &quot;opentron-java-backend-0.1.0-exec.jar&quot;,
  &quot;sha256&quot;: &quot;&lt;64 lowercase hexadecimal characters&gt;&quot;,
  &quot;sbomSha256&quot;: &quot;&lt;64 lowercase hexadecimal characters&gt;&quot;,
  &quot;sourceRevision&quot;: &quot;&lt;7-64 hexadecimal commit characters&gt;&quot;
}
```

Keep the signing private key outside the repository and deployment image.
Sign the raw manifest bytes with an Ed25519 key, then store the raw signature
as Base64 text. The verifier accepts the public key as PEM and rejects
symlinked or oversized inputs. The Java-side API is:

```java
var trustedKey = AzureBuildProvenanceVerifier.loadEd25519PublicKey(publicKeyPemPath);
var verified = new AzureBuildProvenanceVerifier(objectMapper)
        .verify(artifactJar, manifestJsonPath, manifestSignaturePath, trustedKey);
```

This is only an offline/release-stage integrity check. It is not wired into
Azure key release and does not establish that the checked file is the code
currently executing or bind an artifact digest to MAA/TPM evidence.

The Java client and composition helper are under
`org.opentron.backend.security.confidential.gateway.sdk`. The composition
helper only passes sanitized prompt text to the supplied model client:

```java
var gateway = new ConfidentialGatewayClient(
        URI.create(&quot;http://127.0.0.1:8000&quot;),
        System.getenv(&quot;CONFIDENTIAL_GATEWAY_API_TOKEN&quot;),
        HttpClient.newHttpClient(),
        new ObjectMapper(),
        Duration.ofSeconds(20));

var completion = new ConfidentialCompletionClient(gateway, modelClient::complete);
String answer = completion.complete(&quot;Contact ada@example.com&quot;);
```

The model adapter must not log prompts or responses and should use its own
provider authentication and timeout policy. This prototype helper is
non-streaming; it does not automatically protect separate chat history,
retrieval context, or tool arguments unless the caller includes them in the
prompt passed through the Gateway. Use the SDK&apos;s `sanitizeSegments` and
`desanitizeSegments` methods when protecting distinct chat messages or other
separately structured text.

Windows packaging (optional)

You can create a native Windows installer or executable using `jpackage` (JDK
17+). Example:

```bash
cd java/opentron-java/backend/target
jpackage --type exe --input . --name OpentronBackend \
	--main-jar opentron-java-backend-0.1.0.jar --app-version 0.1.0 \
	--icon path/to/icon.ico
```

Alternatively use Launch4j or Inno Setup for more advanced installers.</file></files></repomix>
